20:["$","p",null,{"children":["Meet ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"Rusty"}],", a differential-drive rover with two wheels, two encoders, and a scanning laser.\nRusty is going to be with us for the whole book: ",["$","$L75",null,{"href":"/chapters/ch04-rusty-and-sensors","children":"Chapter 4"}],"\nbuilds it properly, and by ",["$","$L75",null,{"href":"/chapters/ch26-capstone","children":"Chapter 26"}]," it explores and maps an apartment\nit has never seen."]}]
21:["$","p",null,{"children":"Right now Rusty has one job: drive a 2.4 m square and come back to where it started."}]
22:["$","$L76",null,{"children":["$","$L77",null,{}]}]
23:["$","p",null,{"children":"Watch a full lap before reading on. Three things are happening, and only one of them is a surprise."}]
24:["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"The orange path is not an error."}]," It is the pose Rusty computes by integrating its own commands —\n",["$","em",null,{"children":"dead reckoning"}],", the oldest navigation algorithm there is. Drive 0.6 m/s for four seconds, turn\nninety degrees, repeat. The arithmetic is exact, the square closes to within a billionth of a metre\n— there is a test at the end of this chapter that says so — and if you asked Rusty where it was, it\nwould answer with total confidence."]}]
25:["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"The gray path is where Rusty actually went."}]," One wheel had a fraction more traction than the\nother. The turn was 89.4 degrees instead of 90. The floor was a millimetre out of level. Each of\nthose errors is small, unbiased, and entirely ordinary — and each one enters the pose and stays\nthere."]}]
26:["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"The error never comes back down."}]," That is the surprise, and it is worth being precise about why.\nThe individual errors are zero-mean: they are as likely to be positive as negative, so they do not\npush Rusty in any particular direction. But integration accumulates them, and a sum of zero-mean\nerrors is a random walk, whose spread grows without bound. Worse, an error in ",["$","em",null,{"children":"heading"}]," multiplies\nthe distance travelled after it, so a third of a degree at the first corner is centimetres by the\nfourth. Re-roll the seed: the drift is a different size and a different direction every time, and no\namount of staring at the commands will tell you which."]}]

4b:["$","$L74",null,{"as":"h2","id":"what-the-paradigm-buys-and-what-it-costs","children":"What the paradigm buys, and what it costs"}]
4c:["$","p",null,{"children":"The honest version of the sales pitch has two columns."}]
4d:["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"What it buys."}]," Ambiguity becomes representable, so a robot can hold three hypotheses without\npicking one prematurely. Weak models become usable: probabilistic algorithms make far weaker demands\non model accuracy than classical planners do, because they were built expecting the model to be\nwrong. Failure becomes graceful rather than catastrophic — a filter degrades toward ignorance, which\nis recoverable, instead of toward confident error, which is not. And, as the T-junction showed,\ninformation gathering becomes a first-class action rather than an afterthought."]}]
4e:["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"What it costs."}]," Computation, first: carrying a whole distribution is strictly more expensive than\ncarrying a number, and in high dimensions it is exponentially so. Approximation, second: real state\nspaces are continuous, exact posteriors have infinitely many degrees of freedom, and every algorithm\nin this book replaces the true belief with something finite — a Gaussian, a grid, a set of samples.\nThe approximation is where the failures live, which is why every chapter here names the one it is\nmaking."]}]
4f:["$","p",null,{"children":["That trade is why the book is structured as a sequence of representations rather than a single\nalgorithm, and why no chapter is allowed to claim a method is \"better\" without saying what it is\nbetter ",["$","em",null,{"children":"at"}],"."]}]
